--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the foraging mission carefully.

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering the "good" item (one color half of source)
- Score increases by 1 for delivering the "bad" item (other color half)
- We need robots to: find the source, pick up the RIGHT item (one specific color), carry it to the nest, deposit it
- The robot can perceive: floor color (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=middle), wall colors (RED=nest side, BLUE/GREEN=source halves)
- Key insight: robots should go toward BLUE or GREEN (one specific color at source), then go toward RED (nest wall) to return

**Strategy:**
- We don't know which color (BLUE or GREEN) is "good" yet - need to try both
- Basic FSM: Explore → if BLACK floor (source area), go toward RED wall (nest) → if WHITE floor (nest), explore again
- Better: Go toward one source color → carry back to RED wall → repeat

**State designs:**
1. **Explore** → transition to "go to source color" when perceiving BLUE/GREEN
2. **Go to nest (RED)** when on BLACK floor or carrying item
3. **Return** to exploration when on WHITE floor

Key observations:
- GoToColor with RED (color 1) = go to nest wall
- GoToColor with BLUE (color 3) or GREEN (color 2) = go to good source half
- Floor BLACK = in source area (picked up item)
- Floor WHITE = in nest area (deposited item)

I'll try both BLUE and GREEN as target colors, with various topologies.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to guide my decisions. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and the structure of my Round 2 submissions.

**Analysis:**
- The key insight is: robots need to go to ONE specific color half (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2), then return to nest (RED=1 wall)
- Floor BLACK = at source (just picked up item), Floor WHITE = at nest (just delivered)
- We don't know which color is "good" (decreases score) - need both BLUE and GREEN variants
- The simplest effective FSM: State0=GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) → on BLACK floor → State1=GoToColor(RED) → on WHITE floor → State0

**Key improvements to try:**
1. Use GoAwayColor(RED) when at source to help navigate away from nest toward source
2. Try GoAwayColor for the "bad" color to avoid picking up wrong items
3. Vary transition probabilities
4. Mix exploration with directed movement

**Design decisions:**
- Keep both BLUE and GREEN variants (we still don't know which is good)
- Elitism: copy best topologies from Round 2 (all 2-state and 3-state directed variants)
- Exploit: tweak probabilities on promising topologies
- Explore: try GoAwayColor(RED) to push toward source, 4-state machines

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received score feedback, which means I need to reason more carefully about what should work best and diversify aggressively.

**Deep reasoning:**
- Score is MINIMISED: delivering "good" items decreases score (good!), delivering "bad" items increases it (bad!)
- We need to identify which color half (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) gives good items
- Since we can't know without scores, we must try both AND try avoiding the other
- Critical insight: GoAwayColor(RED=1) pushes robots TOWARD source (away from nest wall)
- Critical insight: GoToColor(RED=1) pulls robots TOWARD nest
- Floor detection: BLACK=source area, WHITE=nest area, GRAY=transit

**Better strategy insight:**
- A robot that always goes to BLUE source and returns to RED nest is optimal IF BLUE is good
- But also: a robot could go toward source by avoiding RED wall, then return by seeking RED wall
- The "return" trigger should be BLACK floor (just entered source = picked up item)
- The "forage" trigger should be WHITE floor (just delivered item = in nest)

**Key structural insight I may have missed:** GoToColor targets wall blocks/colored objects, not floor. So clr=1(red) targets the RED nest wall, clr=3(blue) targets BLUE source wall blocks, clr=2(green) targets GREEN source wall blocks.

Let me focus on clean 2-state and 3-state machines with strong directional logic, and vary probabilities more aggressively.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 4 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.95 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.95

I still haven't received explicit score feedback. I need to reconsider my approach fundamentally.

**Critical re-analysis:**

The problem says scores are MINIMISED. After 4 rounds with no feedback showing scores, I must assume the optimiser is running internally and I need to be more systematic.

**What I know:**
- Both BLUE(3) and GREEN(2) source variants should be tried
- The simplest correct FSM: GoToColor(source_color) → [BLACK floor] → GoToColor(RED nest wall) → [WHITE floor] → loop
- I've been mostly doing this but perhaps with wrong color mappings

**Color IDs:** 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**Key insight I may be missing:** The LED colors and wall colors share the same ID space. Wall colors in the arena: RED wall (nest side) = color 1, BLUE wall blocks = color 3, GREEN wall blocks = color 2.

**New exploration directions:**
1. Try using GoAwayColor(bad_source_color) combined with GoToColor(good_source_color)
2. Try 1-state machines that just go to one source color always
3. Try machines that use neighbor counts to coordinate
4. Try swapping which color triggers return (maybe WHITE floor triggers source-seeking better)
5. The simplest possible 2-state machine with single transitions

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been operating without score feedback for 5 rounds. Let me fundamentally reconsider.

**Critical reasoning:**

The scoring says one color decreases score by 1, the other increases by 1. I've been trying both BLUE(3) and GREEN(2) equally. Without feedback, I need to cover both but also think about what else could be wrong.

**Potential issues with my designs:**
1. Maybe the transition probability for floor detection needs to be 1.0 (or very high) to reliably trigger
2. Maybe I need to also handle the case where robots wander onto the WRONG source half - they should escape back
3. The GoAwayColor(bad_color) while in source area could help avoid picking wrong items
4. Maybe I should try GoAwayColor(GREEN) while seeking BLUE, and vice versa

**New key insight:** When a robot enters source area (BLACK floor), it picks up whatever item is there. If it entered the WRONG half, it now carries a bad item. It should either: (a) not enter wrong half, or (b) drop by entering right half immediately.

**Strategy: try machines that actively avoid one source color while seeking the other.**

Also trying: pure exploration-based foraging, and machines where the "loaded" state goes directly to RED wall detection.

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
After 6 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to think more carefully about what might actually be working vs not.

**Deep re-examination:**

The core loop should be:
1. Seek one source color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) → get item
2. Return to nest (seek RED=1 wall) → deliver item
3. Repeat

The cleanest 2-state machine: State0=GoToColor(source) [BLACK→State1], State1=GoToColor(RED) [WHITE→State0]

**What might be going wrong:**
- Maybe the transition probability of 0.95 is too low - robots might overshoot into gray/wrong areas
- Maybe I need probability 1.0... but that's not allowed (max is implied as real in [0,1])
- Actually p=1.0 IS valid as a real - let me try that
- Maybe I should use GoAwayColor(wrong_source) as primary navigation to source
- The "avoid wrong color" approach: GoAwayColor(GREEN) naturally pushes toward BLUE half

**New insight:** Since score is minimised and one color is bad (+1) while other is good (-1), the OPTIMAL strategy is: always go to good color, always return. With 20 robots doing ~10 trips each in 1200 steps, theoretical minimum is around -200.

**Let me try p=1.0 transitions and also try all 4 possible color combos for source avoidance.**

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 4 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 1.0

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
After 7 rounds without score feedback, I need to fundamentally reconsider what might be happening.

**Critical insight I may have missed:**

The problem says "an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1." Without knowing WHICH half is good, I've been guessing. But perhaps the optimizer IS finding scores and converging - I just don't see them. I should trust the process and keep diversifying.

**What I haven't tried enough:**
1. Machines where robots go to source by floor detection alone (no color camera) - just explore until BLACK floor, then go to RED wall
2. Pure exploration-based foraging - some robots just wander and happen to deliver
3. Machines that use GoAwayColor(GREEN) to reach BLUE side specifically (not just GoToColor)
4. The transition from "return" state should maybe use BLACK floor too (to re-trigger at source)

**New structural idea:** What if the "good" item color matches the LED colors robots emit? Robots emit LED color 4(yellow), 5(magenta), 6(cyan) - these don't match wall colors. But other robots with camera C=7 can see these LEDs. This could enable indirect communication.

**Most important:** Try machines where BLACK floor triggers GoToColor(RED) and WHITE floor triggers exploration/GoToColor(source). Also try p=1.0 on all critical transitions.

After 8 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to be more systematic about what I'm missing.

**Fundamental re-examination:**

I've been assuming GoToColor(RED=1) navigates to the nest. But let me reconsider color IDs:
- The problem says LED colors are {0,4,5,6} = {off, yellow, magenta, cyan}
- The clr parameter for GoToColor uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}
- Wall colors: RED wall, BLUE blocks, GREEN blocks
- Color perception C=7 uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}

What if the color mapping is: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan? That's what I assumed.

**Critical new thought:** Maybe I have the nest/source navigation backwards! What if robots need to:
- Go AWAY from RED wall to reach source (RED wall is at NEST end)
- Go TOWARD RED wall to return to nest

That's what I've been doing: GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) goes toward source walls, GoToColor(RED) returns to nest. This seems correct.

**Another thought:** Maybe the issue is that GoToColor navigates toward the COLOR as seen by camera, but the colored objects need to be visible. If robots are already AT the source, they may not see BLUE/GREEN walls well. The floor sensor is more reliable.

**Completely new approach:** What if I use GoAwayColor(RED) to go to source, and GoToColor(RED) to return? This might be simpler and more reliable.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
This is the final round. I need to make my best guesses count.

**Final analysis after 9 rounds:**

I've been submitting two parallel families (BLUE=3 and GREEN=2 source color) with various topologies. Without score feedback, I don't know which color is "good." But the optimizer should have found something by now.

**What I know works logically:**
- The cleanest foraging loop: GoToColor(source) → [BLACK floor] → GoToColor(RED) → [WHITE floor] → repeat
- Using p=1.0 on floor transitions makes the loop most reliable
- GoAwayColor(RED) as alternative to reach source

**Final round strategy:**
- Cover BOTH color variants (BLUE and GREEN) with the most reliable topologies
- Include the GoAwayColor(RED) approach as it's fundamentally different navigation
- Keep 3-state machines with exploration fallback
- Try a machine that combines both source colors (go to BLUE, if wrong go to GREEN)
- Include pure floor-based navigation (no camera for source finding)

**Key insight for final round:** The simplest machines with fewest failure modes should dominate. The 2-state machine with p=1.0 single transitions is the most reliable forager IF the color is correct. Submit multiple variants covering both colors and both navigation strategies.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
